## Semantic Chunks
### Section: Introduction
- **Level**: `intro`
- **Hash**: `4c4de424693500ce6c1eb174bc7adb1fa02e886d0bdf0e3c6f192ea61f2c647d`
- **Best Competitor Match**: `/learning/2026/8/what-evidence-should-teachers-collect-when-they-suspect-ai-use` (Similarity: 0.9375)

```text
When an assignment raises concerns, the questions a teacher asks can make a major difference. A student may have submitted work that is dramatically different from their previous writing, an AI detector may have flagged portions of the text, document history may show large sections appearing at once, citations may be difficult to verify, or the student may struggle to explain the language used in the paper.

None of those signals automatically proves that the student used ChatGPT, another AI tool, or someone else's work. The next step is usually to ask specific, evidence-based questions about the assignment and how it was created .

Instead of beginning with "Did you cheat?" or "Did ChatGPT write this?" teachers can ask students to explain their thesis, sources, writing process, revisions, unusual passages, and any digital tools used.

Checkmark Plagiarism helps teachers prepare for these conversations with AI detection , essay writing playback , static AI detection, plagiarism detection , autograding , and integrations with Canvas and Google Classroom .
```

### Section: Start With an Open-Ended Question
- **Level**: `h2`
- **Hash**: `63b1828d81885b1d48f9f8be363f4a9d9941a5f85aebdb16e3a54b2a318d41ff`
- **Best Competitor Match**: `/learning/2026/8/what-should-i-do-if-a-student-cant-explain-their-own-essay` (Similarity: 0.8517)

```text
One of the best initial questions is: "Can you walk me through how you completed this assignment?" This gives the student an open opportunity to explain their brainstorming, research, drafting, tools, tutoring assistance, and revision workflow before specific concerns are introduced.
```

### Section: Questions About the Writing Process
- **Level**: `h2`
- **Hash**: `da7264108fe23dfb0c423227f13616c6acebfa3d49f72f68524eb5f379682ebe`
- **Best Competitor Match**: `/learning/2026/8/how-do-i-talk-to-a-student-i-suspect-of-using-ai` (Similarity: 0.8495)

```text
Inquire into the concrete mechanics of how the paper came together:

Where did you start when you received the prompt? 
 Did you create an outline or brainstorm notes first? 
 Where did you write your first draft? 
 Which section was most challenging to write? 
 Which paragraph did you revise the most? 
 Did you write any portions in another document before pasting them here? 
 Did anyone provide feedback or editing help?
```

### Section: Questions About the Thesis
- **Level**: `h2`
- **Hash**: `e86c2f8d7351f8cec6b97883530e34d83905e132490795b3aa0fd4ab895d9d6b`
- **Best Competitor Match**: `/learning/2026/8/should-i-ask-a-student-to-explain-their-writing-if-i-suspect-ai` (Similarity: 0.9189)

```text
Ask: "What is the main argument you are making in this paper?" The student does not need to recite the exact formal thesis statement verbatim. Explaining the core thesis in everyday conversational language is strong evidence of authentic conceptual ownership.
```

### Section: Questions About Specific Paragraphs
- **Level**: `h2`
- **Hash**: `f80c18577a26a6f1f6fb763c7ddd495214e0586d1865987dcbd43ae08f5b5533`
- **Best Competitor Match**: `/learning/2026/8/should-i-ask-a-student-to-explain-their-writing-if-i-suspect-ai` (Similarity: 0.8858)

```text
Select central argumentative paragraphs and ask: "Can you explain what you're saying in this paragraph and how it supports your main argument?" Specific questions give students concrete ideas to explain rather than demanding abstract defenses.
```

### Section: Questions About Advanced Vocabulary
- **Level**: `h2`
- **Hash**: `c6650fefdf40e66bcb4816ef10cb3896fa0cbd18acde58f428000ca8fe470ece`
- **Best Competitor Match**: `/learning/2026/8/what-should-i-do-if-a-student-cant-explain-their-own-essay` (Similarity: 0.9309)

```text
If the paper uses terminology not previously seen in the student's work (e.g., "socioeconomic stratification," "institutional marginalization," "cognitive dissonance," or "epistemological limitations" ), ask: "What does this term mean in the context of your argument?" Look for contextual comprehension rather than dictionary definitions.
```

### Section: Questions About Evidence and Examples
- **Level**: `h2`
- **Hash**: `0ba805d719dc2176e3e3aabf54a42a8be5cdb47e74390ed1097dd0be52ea1604`
- **Best Competitor Match**: `/learning/2026/8/what-should-i-do-if-a-student-cant-explain-their-own-essay` (Similarity: 0.8803)

```text
Ask: "Why did you choose this specific quotation? What did this example demonstrate? How did this source shape your conclusion?" Authentic writers are familiar with the evidence supporting their claims.
```

### Section: Questions About Sources
- **Level**: `h2`
- **Hash**: `d6a025dc120ccc1f298b15504482a04db7fcc889228d406f88b50b515fb9825d`
- **Best Competitor Match**: `/learning/2026/8/should-i-ask-a-student-to-explain-their-writing-if-i-suspect-ai` (Similarity: 0.8733)

```text
Ask: "Where did you find this source, and what did it contribute to your paper?" If a citation cannot be located in academic databases, ask neutrally: "I wasn't able to locate this article. Can you show me where you found it?" This allows the student to clarify whether it was an innocent formatting error or a larger issue.
```

### Section: Questions About Revision
- **Level**: `h2`
- **Hash**: `3ac1e0eb7e1c8a19e0da57bee76d4b3e605a413ef6284142e25b32e13e6490d9`
- **Best Competitor Match**: `/learning/2026/8/what-should-i-do-if-a-student-cant-explain-their-own-essay` (Similarity: 0.8410)

```text
Ask: "What changed between your initial rough draft and your final submission? Was there an argument you ended up removing?" Authors almost always remember major structural revisions.
```

### Section: Questions About a Large Text Addition
- **Level**: `h2`
- **Hash**: `cf19e0b608768144dd25aa9ff310a98d1c7e5d7bb46cb0a9308e0d502c63d8f3`
- **Best Competitor Match**: `/learning/2026/8/can-writing-history-show-whether-a-student-used-ai` (Similarity: 0.9035)

```text
If document history shows hundreds of words appearing simultaneously, ask neutrally: "I noticed that this section was added all at once. Can you explain where it came from?" The student may have drafted it in Microsoft Word, Google Docs, or research notes.
```

### Section: Questions About Writing Tools & AI Writing Assistants
- **Level**: `h2`
- **Hash**: `972e852422d1a6265f20f6577ec8b09577f9e792093f5af1b05c0ca9f0d72e07`
- **Best Competitor Match**: `/learning/2026/8/what-should-i-look-for-when-a-students-writing-suddenly-improves` (Similarity: 0.9481)

```text
Ask: "What software, tools, or extensions did you use while writing or revising?" If the student mentions Grammarly or similar tools, ask what the tool actually did: basic spellcheck vs. automated paragraph rewriting. Read more in can AI detectors detect Grammarly or AI writing assistants?
```

### Section: Questions About ChatGPT or Generative AI
- **Level**: `h2`
- **Hash**: `117a121fe90dfd3b75781cafa9e12b748ae69550569cbc80e57df672888eaabc`
- **Best Competitor Match**: `/learning/2026/8/what-should-i-do-if-a-student-cant-explain-their-own-essay` (Similarity: 0.9391)

```text
When relevant, ask directly and neutrally: "Did you use ChatGPT or another AI tool while working on this assignment? What specifically did you use it for?" Distinguish brainstorming from full essay generation.
```

### Section: Do Not Treat Any AI Use as Automatic Cheating
- **Level**: `h2`
- **Hash**: `b887211617b009c8e51f6849f44e577cba4b10cc685c6e03b5c8b1fc8243e30f`
- **Best Competitor Match**: `/learning/2026/8/how-do-i-talk-to-a-student-i-suspect-of-using-ai` (Similarity: 0.9288)

```text
Student A (Permitted Ideation)

"I asked ChatGPT for 5 research topics, chose one, outlined it, and wrote the essay myself."

Permitted exploratory use under many course policies.

Student B (Prohibited Generation)

"I pasted the prompt into ChatGPT and copied the generated paragraphs into my submission."

Violates assignment authorship requirements.
```

### Section: What Questions Should I Avoid?
- **Level**: `h2`
- **Hash**: `ced898647e0a806275d57a36a738779da078e39149ea4e8921934004c97f62f2`
- **Best Competitor Match**: `/learning/2026/8/should-i-ask-a-student-to-explain-their-writing-if-i-suspect-ai` (Similarity: 0.8777)

```text
Avoid accusatory demands: "Why did you cheat?" or "Why don't you just admit you used ChatGPT?" 
 Avoid attacking student capability: "How could you possibly have written this?" 
 Avoid treating software scores as verdicts: "The detector says AI, so what do you have to say for yourself?"
```

### Section: A Practical Question Sequence
- **Level**: `h2`
- **Hash**: `bd243ede12c2d87c287d3cf281aba12383c745742bb65716b50f07498d701283`
- **Best Competitor Match**: `/learning/2026/8/should-i-ask-a-student-to-explain-their-writing-if-i-suspect-ai` (Similarity: 0.8370)

```text
A Practical 10-Step Question Sequence for Student Conferences:

1. Start Broadly: "Can you walk me through how you completed this assignment?" 
 2. Ask About the Thesis: "What is the main point or argument you're making?" 
 3. Discuss the Process: "Where did you draft the paper, and what did you revise most?" 
 4. Inquire About Specific Passages: "Can you explain what you're arguing in this paragraph?" 
 5. Verify Sources: "Where did you find this source, and how did it shape your claim?" 
 6. Address Writing History: "I noticed this large section appeared at once. Where did it come from?" 
 7. Ask About Digital Tools: "What writing tools, extensions, or tutoring assistance did you use?" 
 8. Ask Directly About AI: "Did you use ChatGPT or another AI tool? How specifically was it used?" 
 9. Request Supporting Materials: "Do you have earlier drafts, outlines, or research notes?" 
 10. Outline Next Steps: Explain the school's review procedure transparently if concerns persist.
```

### Section: Ask Questions That Help Explain the Evidence
- **Level**: `h2`
- **Hash**: `1da3b6ec6c34be402ec569bab26b29131bddd59b45410155ec62e494bb22072e`

The goal of questioning a student is not to secure a confession—it is to understand the authentic drafting process behind the paper. By pairing targeted inquiries with writing playback, citation verification, and AI detection, teachers can reach fair and defensible academic integrity outcomes.

Checkmark Plagiarism supports that process with AI detection, essay writing playback, static AI detection, plagiarism detection, autograding, and integrations with Canvas and Google Classroom.
